Source code: 
```c
float s13110(int iterations,float aa[256][256])
{
    int xindex, yindex;
    float max, chksum;
    for (int nl = 0; nl < 100*(iterations/(256)); nl++) {
        max = aa[(0)][0];
        xindex = 0;
        yindex = 0;
        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            for (int j = 0; j < 256; j++) {
                if (aa[i][j] > max) {
                    max = aa[i][j];
                    xindex = i;
                    yindex = j;
                }
            }
        }
        chksum = max + (float) xindex + (float) yindex;
    }
    return max + xindex+1 + yindex+1;
}
```

Optimized code:
```c

float s13110_opt(int iterations, float aa[256][256]) {
    int xindex, yindex;
    float max, chksum;
    int outer_iters = 100 * (iterations / 256);
    
    for (int nl = 0; nl < outer_iters; nl++) {
        float row_max[256];
        int row_yindex[256];
        
        // First pass: find max in each row (vectorizable reduction)
        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            float local_max = aa[i][0];
            for (int j = 1; j < 256; j++) {
                float current_val = aa[i][j];
                local_max = current_val > local_max ? current_val : local_max;
            }
            row_max[i] = local_max;
        }
        
        // Second pass: find column index of max in each row (no early break)
        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            float target_max = row_max[i];
            int last_idx = 0;
            // Scan entire row, tracking last occurrence
            for (int j = 0; j < 256; j++) {
                if (aa[i][j] == target_max) {
                    last_idx = j;
                }
            }
            row_yindex[i] = last_idx;
        }
        
        // Third pass: find max across rows (vectorizable reduction)
        float final_max = row_max[0];
        for (int i = 1; i < 256; i++) {
            final_max = row_max[i] > final_max ? row_max[i] : final_max;
        }
        
        // Fourth pass: find row index of max (no early break)
        int max_row_idx = 0;
        // Scan all rows, tracking last occurrence
        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            if (row_max[i] == final_max) {
                max_row_idx = i;
            }
        }
        
        max = final_max;
        xindex = max_row_idx;
        yindex = row_yindex[max_row_idx];
        chksum = max + (float)xindex + (float)yindex;
    }
    
    return max + xindex + 1 + yindex + 1;
}

```

## Analysis

### 1. Compilation Errors in Optimized Code
The optimized code compiles successfully (no compilation errors reported).

### 2. Semantic Comparison with Simple Test Cases

**Critical Issue Found**: The optimized code has different semantics from the source code.

**Problem 1: Different index selection logic**
- Source code: When multiple elements have the same maximum value, it selects the **first occurrence** (due to `if (aa[i][j] > max)` condition).
- Optimized code: When multiple elements have the same maximum value, it selects the **last occurrence** within a row (due to `last_idx = j` assignment without early break) and the **last occurrence** across rows (due to `max_row_idx = i` assignment without early break).

**Example verification**:
Consider a 2x2 array for simplicity:
```
aa[0][0] = 5.0, aa[0][1] = 5.0
aa[1][0] = 5.0, aa[1][1] = 5.0
```
- Source code: Finds max=5.0 at i=0, j=0 (first occurrence)
- Optimized code: Finds max=5.0 at i=1, j=1 (last occurrence in last row)

**Problem 2: Incorrect final return value**
- Source code returns: `max + xindex+1 + yindex+1`
- Optimized code returns: `max + xindex + 1 + yindex + 1` (same expression, but with different xindex/yindex values)

**Problem 3: Unnecessary complexity**
The optimized code performs 4 passes over the data instead of 1, increasing memory accesses and potentially hurting performance despite vectorization.

**Result**: Semantic comparison **FAILS** - the optimized code does not produce identical results to the source code.

### 3. Vectorization Analysis (Not Applicable Due to Semantic Issues)
Since the semantics differ, we cannot proceed to vectorization analysis. The optimized code needs to be corrected first.

### 4. Optimization Opportunities in Original Code
The original code's main loop has:
- True dependency: `max`, `xindex`, `yindex` are loop-carried dependencies
- These prevent vectorization because each iteration depends on the previous comparison result
- However, this is a **reduction pattern** (finding maximum value and its location) that could be transformed

### 5. Suggested Fixes
To maintain correct semantics while enabling optimization:
1. Track both value and indices during reduction
2. Use conditional selection that preserves first-occurrence semantics
3. Consider using SIMD intrinsics or OpenMP reduction for parallel maximum finding
4. The reduction could be split into independent row reductions followed by a final reduction

1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.

     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

**3. Vectorization Analysis**

The compiler feedback indicates:
- Inner loop (j-loop): "value that could not be identified as reduction is used outside the loop"
- Outer reduction loop (i-loop): Same issue

The problem is that the reduction tracks **three values** (max, xindex, yindex), but vectorization requires the reduction to produce a **single value** or a set of values that can be reduced independently.

**Why vectorization fails:**
1. The reduction variables (local_max, local_yindex) have a **coupled dependency** - when we update local_max, we must also update local_yindex
2. This creates a **non-vectorizable reduction pattern** because the index update depends on the comparison result
3. The compiler cannot automatically vectorize reductions with multiple dependent variables

**Optimization Opportunity:**
The current optimization attempts to enable vectorization by splitting the 2D search into row-wise then column-wise reductions, but still has the coupled max/index dependency. To enable vectorization, we need to:
1. Separate the max-finding from index-tracking, or
2. Use a different algorithm that allows independent reductions

**FAIL** - The optimized code has optimization opportunities (vectorization is not achieved due to coupled dependencies in reduction).

   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments

     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```

    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;

     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:

     // After reorder:

     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:

     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }

     ```

   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops

```c
float s13110_opt(int iterations, float aa[256][256]) {
    int xindex, yindex;
    float max, chksum;
    int outer_iters = 100 * (iterations / 256);
    
    for (int nl = 0; nl < outer_iters; nl++) {
        float row_max[256];
        int row_yindex[256];
        
        // First pass: find max in each row (vectorizable reduction)
        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            float local_max = aa[i][0];
            int local_yindex = 0;
            
            // Process elements in chunks to enable vectorization
            for (int j = 1; j < 256; j++) {
                float current_val = aa[i][j];
                // Use conditional move pattern (compiler may optimize to max instruction)
                if (current_val > local_max) {
                    local_max = current_val;
                    local_yindex = j;
                }
            }
            row_max[i] = local_max;
            row_yindex[i] = local_yindex;
        }
        
        // Second pass: find max across rows (vectorizable reduction)
        float final_max = row_max[0];
        int final_xindex = 0;
        
        for (int i = 1; i < 256; i++) {
            if (row_max[i] > final_max) {
                final_max = row_max[i];
                final_xindex = i;
            }
        }
        
        max = final_max;
        xindex = final_xindex;
        yindex = row_yindex[final_xindex];
        chksum = max + (float)xindex + (float)yindex;
    }
    
    return max + xindex + 1 + yindex + 1;
}

**2. Semantic comparison with simple test cases**  

The original code finds the maximum value in a 256×256 array, along with its coordinates `(xindex, yindex)`.  
The optimized version splits this into two passes:  
- First pass: find max in each row, store row max and its column index.  
- Second pass: find max across rows, then retrieve column index from that row’s stored index.

This is semantically equivalent to the original because:  
- The original’s `max` and `xindex`, `yindex` are updated only when a strictly greater value is found.  
- The two-pass method finds the same maximum value and, for the first occurrence of that maximum in row-major order, the same coordinates.

Optimized: row_max[0]=2, row_yindex[0]=1; row_max[1]=4, row_yindex[1]=1 → final_max=4, final_xindex=1, yindex=1 ✅

Original: max=5, xindex=0, yindex=0 (first occurrence in row-major order)

Semantics match.

**3. Vectorization analysis**  

Compiler feedback says:  
- Inner loop `for (int j = 1; j < 256; j++)` not vectorized because “value that could not be identified as reduction is used outside the loop” — here `local_yindex` is updated conditionally, which is not a standard max reduction (reduction variable must be scalar with associative operation; index tracking breaks pure reduction).  
- Outer loop `for (int i = 1; i < 256; i++)` not vectorized for similar reason (tracking `final_xindex`).

Thus, automatic vectorization fails because of index tracking.  

To make vectorization possible:  
- For the row max: compute max value using vectorized max operations, then do a separate pass to find the first index of that max in the row (or keep track via vector of indices, but that’s more complex).  
- For the across-rows max: similarly, compute max of row_max values vectorially, then find index.

Current code still has optimization opportunity: we can separate the max-finding from index-finding to allow vectorization of the max reduction.

---

**4. Conclusion**  

The optimized code is semantically correct but not fully optimized because:  
- It fails automatic vectorization due to index tracking inside the reduction.  
- There is room to improve by splitting max value reduction and index lookup into separate steps.

    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

If the access pattern involves using a[i-1] and updating a[i] within the same iteration, a dependency exists. Attempt to reorder the instructions to resolve this dependency.

float s13110_opt(int iterations, float aa[256][256]) {
    int xindex, yindex;
    float max, chksum;
    int outer_iters = 100 * (iterations / 256);
    
    for (int nl = 0; nl < outer_iters; nl++) {
        float row_max[256];
        int row_yindex[256];
        
        // First pass: find max in each row (split max value and index tracking)
        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            // Vectorizable max reduction (value only)
            float local_max = aa[i][0];
            for (int j = 1; j < 256; j++) {
                float current_val = aa[i][j];
                local_max = (current_val > local_max) ? current_val : local_max;
            }
            row_max[i] = local_max;
            
            // Find first occurrence of max in this row (separate pass)
            int local_yindex = 0;
            for (int j = 0; j < 256; j++) {
                if (aa[i][j] == local_max) {
                    local_yindex = j;
                    break;
                }
            }
            row_yindex[i] = local_yindex;
        }
        
        // Second pass: find max across rows (split max value and index tracking)
        float final_max = row_max[0];
        for (int i = 1; i < 256; i++) {
            final_max = (row_max[i] > final_max) ? row_max[i] : final_max;
        }
        
        // Find first row containing final_max
        int final_xindex = 0;
        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            if (row_max[i] == final_max) {
                final_xindex = i;
                break;
            }
        }
        
        max = final_max;
        xindex = final_xindex;
        yindex = row_yindex[final_xindex];
        chksum = max + (float)xindex + (float)yindex;
    }
    
    return max + xindex + 1 + yindex + 1;
}

### **2. Semantic Comparison**
The optimized code **does not preserve the semantics** of the original source code.  
**Reason:**  
- The original code finds the **first occurrence** of the maximum value in row-major order (i = 0..255, j = 0..255).  
- The optimized code finds the **first occurrence of the maximum in each row** (j = 0..255), but when multiple rows have the same maximum value, it picks the **first row** containing that value, **not** the first occurrence in row-major order.  
- Example:  
  Suppose `aa[0][1] = 10.0` and `aa[1][0] = 10.0` (both equal max).  
  - Original: `max = aa[0][1]`, `xindex = 0`, `yindex = 1`.  
  - Optimized: It finds `row_max[0] = 10.0`, `row_max[1] = 10.0`. In the second pass, it picks `final_xindex = 0` (first row with max), then `yindex = row_yindex[0] = 1`.  
  This matches in this case, but fails if the first occurrence is **not** the first row containing the max.

**Counterexample:**  
Let `aa[1][0] = 10.0` (max), `aa[0][255] = 9.0`, all others smaller.  
- Original: Scans row 0 first, finds max = 9.0, xindex=0, yindex=255. Then in row 1, finds 10.0 > 9.0, updates max=10.0, xindex=1, yindex=0.  
- Optimized: `row_max[0] = 9.0`, `row_max[1] = 10.0`. Second pass picks `final_max = 10.0`, `final_xindex = 1`, `yindex = row_yindex[1] = 0`.  
This matches here, but the problem is **when two rows have the same max value** and the first occurrence is in a later row but earlier column.

**Critical failure case:**  
`aa[0][5] = 10.0`, `aa[1][2] = 10.0` (same max).  
- Original: Scans (0,0)..(0,5): finds max=10.0 at (0,5). Never updates because no larger value found later. Returns xindex=0, yindex=5.  
- Optimized: `row_max[0] = 10.0`, `row_max[1] = 10.0`. Second pass picks `final_xindex = 0` (first row with max), `yindex = row_yindex[0] = 5`.  
This matches, but only because it picks first row with max.  

Now swap: `aa[0][5] = 10.0`, `aa[1][2] = 10.0`, but `aa[0][5]` is **not** the first occurrence in row-major order? Wait, row-major: (0,5) comes before (1,2) because row index increases after all columns in previous row. So (0,5) is first.  
Actually, the original code’s first occurrence is **row-major order**, so if max appears first in row 0 col 5, that’s the answer. The optimized code picks first row with max (row 0), then first occurrence in that row (col 5). That matches.  

But if max appears first in **row 1 col 2** and also in row 0 col 5? Impossible — row 0 scanned before row 1, so if row 0 has max at col 5, that’s first. So maybe the optimized code is semantically correct?  

Let’s test:  
`aa[0][0] = 1.0`, `aa[0][1] = 2.0`, `aa[1][0] = 2.0`.  
Original:  
- i=0, j=0: max=1.0, x=0, y=0  
- i=0, j=1: max=2.0, x=0, y=1  
- i=1, j=0: aa[1][0]=2.0 not > 2.0, so no update.  
Result: x=0, y=1.  

Optimized:  
row_max[0] = 2.0, row_yindex[0] = 1  
row_max[1] = 2.0, row_yindex[1] = 0  
Second pass: final_max=2.0, final_xindex=0 (first row with max), yindex=1.  
Matches.  

So maybe the optimized code **is** semantically correct because it picks first row with max, then first occurrence in that row, which matches row-major first occurrence.  

But wait — what if row 0 has max at col 5, row 1 has max at col 2, but row 0’s max is **smaller** than row 1’s max? Then original picks row 1 col 2. Optimized picks row 1 col 2. Matches.  

Actually, the only difference could be if the max in a row is **not unique** in that row, and the original picks the first in that row, but optimized picks the first in that row (same). So it seems okay.  

But the unit test says PASS, so semantics are preserved.

**Key issue:**  
The loops that search for the **first index** of a known value (lines 28 and 45) cannot be vectorized because they exit early when found. This is a **loop-carried dependency** on the found index.

**Optimization opportunity:**  
We can improve by removing these non-vectorizable loops.  
- For finding first occurrence of max in a row: we can track index during the max reduction (original code does this).  
- For finding first row with final_max: we can track index during the row-max reduction.

Thus, the current optimized code is **not fully optimized** because it has non-vectorizable loops that could be eliminated.

**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

float s13110_opt(int iterations, float aa[256][256]) {
    int xindex, yindex;
    float max, chksum;
    int outer_iters = 100 * (iterations / 256);
    
    for (int nl = 0; nl < outer_iters; nl++) {
        float row_max[256];
        int row_xindex[256];
        int row_yindex[256];
        
        // First pass: find max and its first occurrence in each row (vectorizable)
        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            float local_max = aa[i][0];
            int local_yindex = 0;
            
            // Vectorizable max reduction with index tracking
            for (int j = 1; j < 256; j++) {
                float current_val = aa[i][j];
                int update = (current_val > local_max);
                local_max = update ? current_val : local_max;
                local_yindex = update ? j : local_yindex;
            }
            
            row_max[i] = local_max;
            row_xindex[i] = i;
            row_yindex[i] = local_yindex;
        }
        
        // Second pass: find max across rows (vectorizable)
        float final_max = row_max[0];
        int final_idx = 0;
        
        for (int i = 1; i < 256; i++) {
            int update = (row_max[i] > final_max);
            final_max = update ? row_max[i] : final_max;
            final_idx = update ? i : final_idx;
        }
        
        max = final_max;
        xindex = row_xindex[final_idx];
        yindex = row_yindex[final_idx];
        chksum = max + (float)xindex + (float)yindex;
    }
    
    return max + xindex + 1 + yindex + 1;
}

float s13110_opt(int iterations, float aa[256][256]) {
    int xindex, yindex;
    float max, chksum;
    int outer_iters = 100 * (iterations / 256);
    
    for (int nl = 0; nl < outer_iters; nl++) {
        float row_max[256];
        int row_yindex[256];
        
        // First pass: find max and its first occurrence in each row (vectorizable)
        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            float local_max = aa[i][0];
            int local_yindex = 0;
            
            // Vectorizable max reduction - separate max finding from index tracking
            for (int j = 1; j < 256; j++) {
                float current_val = aa[i][j];
                if (current_val > local_max) {
                    local_max = current_val;
                }
            }
            
            // Find first occurrence of max value in this row
            // This can be vectorized as a search loop
            for (int j = 0; j < 256; j++) {
                if (aa[i][j] == local_max) {
                    local_yindex = j;
                    break;
                }
            }
            
            row_max[i] = local_max;
            row_yindex[i] = local_yindex;
        }
        
        // Second pass: find max across rows (vectorizable)
        float final_max = row_max[0];

        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            float local_max = aa[i][0];

            // Vectorizable max reduction - separate max finding from index tracking
            for (int j = 1; j < 256; j++) {

        // Second pass: find max across rows (vectorizable)

## Analysis

### 1. Compilation Errors
The optimized code compiles successfully (no compilation errors).

### 2. Semantic Comparison
**Critical Issue Found**: The optimized code has different semantics from the source code.

**Problem**: The source code finds the **first occurrence** of the maximum value (using `>` comparison), while the optimized code finds the **first occurrence of the maximum value within each row** separately, then finds the **first row containing the global maximum**. This can yield different `(xindex, yindex)` results when there are multiple occurrences of the same maximum value.

**Example**:
Consider a 2×2 matrix for simplicity:
```
aa[0][0] = 5.0, aa[0][1] = 5.0
aa[1][0] = 5.0, aa[1][1] = 4.0
```
- **Source code**: Scans row-major order (i=0,j=0 → i=0,j=1 → i=1,j=0 → i=1,j=1). First max=5.0 at (0,0). Since subsequent values (5.0) are not **greater than** current max (5.0), indices remain (0,0).
- **Optimized code**: 
  1. Row 0: max=5.0, first occurrence at j=0 → row_yindex[0]=0
  2. Row 1: max=5.0, first occurrence at j=0 → row_yindex[1]=0
  3. Global max=5.0, first row with max=5.0 is row 0 → (0,0)
  
In this case, both give same result. But consider:
```
aa[0][0] = 4.0, aa[0][1] = 5.0
aa[1][0] = 5.0, aa[1][1] = 4.0
```
- **Source code**: Finds max=5.0 first at (0,1) → stops updating indices for subsequent 5.0 at (1,0).
- **Optimized code**: 
  1. Row 0: max=5.0 at j=1 → row_yindex[0]=1
  2. Row 1: max=5.0 at j=0 → row_yindex[1]=0
  3. Global max=5.0, first row with max=5.0 is row 0 → (0,1)
  
Still same. But the issue arises when we need to track the **first occurrence in row-major order** across the entire matrix, not per-row then across rows.

**Worst-case example**:
```
aa[0][0] = 4.0, aa[0][1] = 4.0
aa[1][0] = 5.0, aa[1][1] = 4.0
```
- **Source code**: Scans (0,0)=4.0, (0,1)=4.0, (1,0)=5.0 → max=5.0 at (1,0)
- **Optimized code**:
  1. Row 0: max=4.0 at j=0 → row_yindex[0]=0
  2. Row 1: max=5.0 at j=0 → row_yindex[1]=0
  3. Global max=5.0, first row with max=5.0 is row 1 → (1,0)
  
Same result again. Actually, the semantic difference is subtle but exists in edge cases with equal maximum values in multiple rows.

**True semantic difference case**:
```
aa[0][0] = 4.0, aa[0][1] = 5.0
aa[1][0] = 5.0, aa[1][1] = 4.0
```
Wait, we already checked this. Let me trace more carefully:

Actually, the optimized code's approach of finding first occurrence per row then first row with global max **does match** the row-major order search when the global max appears in multiple rows. Because:
- Source: scans all cells in row-major order, finds first max at earliest (row,column).
- Optimized: finds first max in each row (column-wise), then picks earliest row with that max value.

These are equivalent because if global max appears first at (r,c) in row-major order, then:
1. In row r, the first occurrence of max is at column ≤ c (could be earlier column in same row)
2. No earlier row has max (otherwise that would be first)
3. So optimized picks row r, and within row r picks earliest column with max (which is ≤ c)

Actually, if in row r, max appears first at column c' < c, then (r,c') would be earlier than (r,c) in row-major order, contradicting that (r,c) was first. So c' = c.

Thus the algorithms are semantically equivalent for finding first occurrence of max in row-major order.

**Unit Test Passed**: The unit test indicates the output is consistent, confirming semantic equivalence.

**Current limitations**:

### Conclusion
The optimized code:
1. Compiles successfully ✓
2. Has correct semantics (unit test passes) ✓  
3. Has partial vectorization but not fully vectorized due to index-finding loops ✗
4. Has optimization opportunities for further improvement

**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

    int xindex, yindex;

    int outer_iters = 100 * (iterations / 256);
    
    for (int nl = 0; nl < outer_iters; nl++) {

                float current_val = aa[i][j];
                if (current_val > local_max) {
                    local_max = current_val;
                }

            // Find first occurrence of max value - restructured to avoid early break
            int found_idx = 0;
            for (int j = 0; j < 256; j++) {
                // Use conditional assignment without break for better vectorization

            row_yindex[i] = found_idx;

        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            // Use conditional assignment without break

            final_idx = is_match ? i : final_idx;

        xindex = final_idx;
        yindex = row_yindex[final_idx];
        chksum = max + (float)xindex + (float)yindex;

float s13110_opt(int iterations, float aa[256][256]) {
    int xindex, yindex;

            for (int j = 1; j < 256; j++) {
                float current_val = aa[i][j];
                if (current_val > local_max) {

                }
            }
            
            // Find first occurrence of max value - restructured to avoid early break
            int found_idx = 0;
            for (int j = 0; j < 256; j++) {
                // Use conditional assignment without break for better vectorization
                int is_match = (aa[i][j] == local_max) && (found_idx == 0);
                found_idx = is_match ? j : found_idx;
            }
            
            row_max[i] = local_max;
            row_yindex[i] = found_idx;
        }
        
        // Second pass: find max across rows
        float final_max = row_max[0];
        
        // Vectorizable reduction to find global max value
        for (int i = 1; i < 256; i++) {
            if (row_max[i] > final_max) {
                final_max = row_max[i];
            }

        // Find first row with global max - restructured to avoid early break

            // Use conditional assignment without break

            final_idx = is_match ? i : final_idx;

        max = final_max;
        xindex = final_idx;
        yindex = row_yindex[final_idx];

    }

}

**Analysis Report**

---

### **1. Compilation Errors in Optimized Code**
The optimized code compiles successfully (no compilation errors reported).  
Proceeding to semantic comparison.

### **2. Semantic Comparison with Simple Test Cases**

The optimized code **does not preserve the semantics** of the original source code.  
The main issue is in how the **first occurrence** of the maximum value is identified.

**Original code behavior:**
- Finds the **first occurrence** of the maximum value in row-major order (i → j).
- If multiple elements have the same maximum value, the earliest in traversal order is chosen.

**Optimized code behavior:**
- First finds the maximum value per row (vectorizable reduction).
- Then finds the **first occurrence** of that maximum **within each row**.
- Then finds the **first row** containing the global maximum.
- However, if multiple rows have the same global maximum value, the **first row** in traversal order is chosen, but this may **not correspond to the first occurrence in row-major order** across the entire array.

**Example to illustrate the issue:**

Consider a 2×2 array (scaled down for simplicity):

```
aa[0][0] = 1.0, aa[0][1] = 3.0
aa[1][0] = 3.0, aa[1][1] = 2.0
```

**Original code traversal order:**
- i=0, j=0: max=1.0, x=0, y=0
- i=0, j=1: max=3.0, x=0, y=1
- i=1, j=0: value 3.0 equals max, but since max already found earlier at (0,1), indices don’t change.
- i=1, j=1: no change

Result: max=3.0, xindex=0, yindex=1.

**Optimized code:**
- Row 0: max=3.0, first occurrence at j=1.
- Row 1: max=3.0, first occurrence at j=0.
- Global max=3.0, first row with this max is row 0.
- Final: xindex=0, yindex=1.

In this case, it matches, but consider:

```
aa[0][0] = 3.0, aa[0][1] = 2.0
aa[1][0] = 3.0, aa[1][1] = 1.0
```

**Original:**  
First max at (0,0), x=0, y=0.

**Optimized:**  
Row 0: max=3.0, first occurrence j=0.  
Row 1: max=3.0, first occurrence j=0.  
Global max=3.0, first row with max=3.0 is row 0 → x=0, y=0.  
Matches again.

Now consider:

```
aa[0][0] = 2.0, aa[0][1] = 3.0
aa[1][0] = 3.0, aa[1][1] = 1.0
```

**Original:**  
First max at (0,1), x=0, y=1.

**Optimized:**  
Row 0: max=3.0, first occurrence j=1.  
Row 1: max=3.0, first occurrence j=0.  
Global max=3.0, first row with max=3.0 is row 0 → x=0, y=1.  
Matches.

But the problem arises when the **first occurrence of global max is in a later row, but an earlier row also has the same max value but not first in row-major order** — actually, that’s impossible because row-major order scans rows sequentially. If row 0 has the max anywhere, the first occurrence will be in row 0. So maybe the algorithm is actually correct for first occurrence? Wait — let’s check:

If row 0 has max=3.0 at j=5, and row 1 has max=3.0 at j=0, the first occurrence in row-major is still in row 0 at j=5. The optimized code picks row 0 correctly. So maybe it’s fine?  

But the **real bug** is in the “find first occurrence of max in row” loop:

```c
int found_idx = 0;
for (int j = 0; j < 256; j++) {
    int is_match = (aa[i][j] == local_max) && (found_idx == 0);
    found_idx = is_match ? j : found_idx;
}
```

If `local_max` equals `aa[i][0]`, then `found_idx` stays 0 (correct).  
But if `local_max` is not at j=0, this loop still works.  
However, `found_idx` is initialized to 0, which is wrong if the max is not at j=0 — but the condition `(found_idx == 0)` ensures it only sets on first match. That’s fine.

Actually, the semantic mismatch is **not** in the “first occurrence” logic, but in the **global max reduction** logic:  
The optimized code finds the global max by comparing `row_max[i]`, but if two rows have the same max, it picks the first row (correct for first occurrence in row-major). So it seems okay.

But the **compiler feedback** says some loops are not vectorized due to “value that could not be identified as reduction is used outside the loop” — that’s the `found_idx` and `final_idx` loops. Those are **search loops**, not reductions, so they can’t be vectorized easily. That’s fine.

However, the **unit test says PASS**, so maybe the semantics are actually preserved.  
But given the algorithm change, there’s a subtlety: The original code updates `xindex` and `yindex` **only when a new strictly greater max is found**. If the same max appears again later, indices don’t change. That means it records the **first occurrence of the maximum value**.

The optimized code finds the first occurrence per row, then picks the first row with the global max. That matches the first occurrence in row-major order. So it’s correct.

Given the unit test passes, semantics are preserved.

---

### **3. Vectorization Analysis**

The compiler feedback shows:
- Loop at line 22 (finding max per row) is vectorized ✓.
- Loop at line 31 (finding first occurrence of max in row) is **not vectorized** (search loop with dependency).
- Loop at line 45 (finding global max across rows) is vectorized ✓.
- Loop at line 53 (finding first row with global max) is **not vectorized** (search loop with dependency).

The non-vectorized loops are **inherently sequential** due to the “first occurrence” search.  
However, this is **not a performance problem** because:
1. They are short (256 iterations).
2. The heavy work is in the max reduction loops, which are vectorized.

**Optimization opportunity:**  
The search loops could be removed if we don’t need the first occurrence but just any occurrence. But the original code requires first occurrence, so we must keep them.

We could try to vectorize the search by using parallel comparison and a priority encoder, but that’s not straightforward in C without intrinsics.

Given the constraints, the code is **already well-optimized**:  
- The hot loops (max reductions) are vectorized.
- The search loops are minimal overhead.

No further optimization is needed without changing the algorithm.

---

- Semantics preserved (unit test passes).
- Vectorization achieved for the computationally intensive parts.
- Remaining non-vectorized loops are necessary for correctness and are low overhead.

```c

float s13110_opt(int iterations, float aa[256][256]) {
    int xindex, yindex;
    float max, chksum;
    int outer_iters = 100 * (iterations / 256);
    
    for (int nl = 0; nl < outer_iters; nl++) {
        float row_max[256];
        int row_yindex[256];
        
        // First pass: find max and its first occurrence in each row
        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            float local_max = aa[i][0];
            int local_yindex = 0;
            
            // Vectorizable max reduction - find max value in row
            for (int j = 1; j < 256; j++) {
                float current_val = aa[i][j];
                if (current_val > local_max) {
                    local_max = current_val;
                }
            }
            
            // Find first occurrence of max value - restructured to avoid early break
            int found_idx = 0;
            for (int j = 0; j < 256; j++) {
                // Use conditional assignment without break for better vectorization
                int is_match = (aa[i][j] == local_max) && (found_idx == 0);
                found_idx = is_match ? j : found_idx;
            }
            
            row_max[i] = local_max;
            row_yindex[i] = found_idx;
        }
        
        // Second pass: find max across rows
        float final_max = row_max[0];
        
        // Vectorizable reduction to find global max value
        for (int i = 1; i < 256; i++) {
            if (row_max[i] > final_max) {
                final_max = row_max[i];
            }
        }
        
        // Find first row with global max - restructured to avoid early break
        int final_idx = 0;
        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            // Use conditional assignment without break
            int is_match = (row_max[i] == final_max) && (final_idx == 0);
            final_idx = is_match ? i : final_idx;
        }
        
        max = final_max;
        xindex = final_idx;
        yindex = row_yindex[final_idx];
        chksum = max + (float)xindex + (float)yindex;
    }
    
    return max + xindex + 1 + yindex + 1;
}
